# EXPANSION CW125-04 — Their Share

## A prose-first memorial expansion about the remembered claim, the people who inherit it, and the blanks that remain human.

### Prose Wave 125: What a Warning Cannot Finish

## Batch brief

**Content type:** original game-content proposal with full scene, attributable writing, conversation, and conditional-return drafts.
**Content bank:** 48 beat pairings; four alternative authored forms for each pairing; 192 candidates, of which a verified consumer should select only what it can support.
**Current local anchor:** memorial_food_shortage/their_share — Food Shortage Victims.
**Tone:** A memorial about hunger, collective memory, and the risk of turning an unverified account of sacrifice into a clean story that asks the living to accept deprivation.
**Scope:** narrative prose only; no production code, authoritative JSON edits, mechanics, route, quest, flag, simulation, or save change.

## 1. Expansion thesis

This plan explores A memorial about hunger, collective memory, and the risk of turning an unverified account of sacrifice into a clean story that asks the living to accept deprivation. It uses the source text “They starved so others could eat. When the rations ran out, these survivors gave up their share. We will never forget their sacrifice.” as a narrow canon anchor, not as an excuse to manufacture witnesses or explain every silence. Six movements and forty-eight beat pairings explore how different present readers may respond to the wording. The proposed passages remain optional editorial drafts until a current owner and route are verified.

## 2. Story question

How can the story respect the memorial’s claim about a surrendered share without declaring that hunger was noble, chosen, or fairly distributed?

## 5. Human center

The unresolved distance between “they starved” and “gave up their share,” and the people who must live with both sentences.
 The deceased or collective subject must never become a device whose only purpose is to teach a clean lesson to the living. Keep the person or people larger than the inscription’s most repeatable line.

## 6. Voice and point of view

- **A keeper of the shared table:** Resists turning other people’s hunger into a lesson for the living.
- **A reader who needs the tribute:** Takes comfort from the claimed generosity while admitting the wording cannot prove the whole story.
- **A younger listener:** Asks who decided what counted as “their share.”

The people listed here are proposed present-day readers, not canon characters and not witnesses unless another verified source establishes that history. Keep the original memorial voice distinct from any reply written for this plan.

## 7. Placement and current reachability

The exact local anchor is memorial_food_shortage/their_share. MemorialSystem owns survivor memorialization, grief, mourning, and eulogies, but this separate data record’s loader and player-facing consumer have not been verified. Before content placement, confirm the current schema, owner, route, and presentation context. Catalog presence alone proves no playable route. This plan contributes no new route or content registry.

## 8. Player agency

The player may read, ask who authored a claim, hear a second interpretation, decline to discuss it, or leave without a verdict. These are editorial postures rather than promised controls. Silence must remain a complete response. Do not attach approval, trust, reputation, item, resource, treatment, memorial completion, or relationship state to the reader’s interpretation.

## 9. Continuity, dignity, and safety

Do not invent individual names, ration quantities, causes, duration, policy, food type, health effect, or proof of voluntary choice. Do not romanticize starvation, offer nutrition advice, or imply that memorial praise authorizes future deprivation. The record does not specify a food system or a playable choice.
 Keep the source statement, a proposed speaker’s interpretation, and any verified world fact visibly separate. Avoid reenacting death, assigning fault without evidence, or converting grief into a tutorial.

## 11. Narrative sequence

These six editorial movements organize the prose bank; they are not six required visits, a chronology, or a quest chain. A verified consumer may use a single beat, a short excerpt, or none of the candidates.

### Movement 1: The Sentence Starts with Hunger

The first verb is bodily and direct, not ceremonial. In this movement the candidate question is: What happens when the memorial begins with an outcome rather than a choice? The memorial’s phrase “starved” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 2: Others Could Eat

A collective benefit is stated, but its distribution remains undescribed. In this movement the candidate question is: Who are the “others” in the sentence? The memorial’s phrase “so others could eat” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 3: When the Rations Ran Out

The source gives an ending condition but no time or system details. In this movement the candidate question is: How can scarcity be remembered without becoming a planning lesson? The memorial’s phrase “the rations ran out” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 4: The Word Survivors

The memorial calls the dead survivors, a status that does not recover names. In this movement the candidate question is: What does that label preserve, and what does it flatten? The memorial’s phrase “these survivors” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 5: Gave Up Their Share

The text presents a sacrifice whose agency and terms remain unverified. In this movement the candidate question is: What care is owed before repeating this as a voluntary choice? The memorial’s phrase “gave up” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 6: Never Forget

A promise of memory sits beside the risk of asking others to imitate the dead. In this movement the candidate question is: How can remembrance refuse to become a demand? The memorial’s phrase “their share” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

## 12. Beat bank and alternative passage drafts

Every movement meets each of eight source phrases. The four forms per pairing are alternatives: an observable scene, an attributable reader-authored fragment, a two-voice conversation, and a conditional return. Their headings help writers compare form; they do not promise that all versions fit one consumer. Dialogue is deliberately from proposed present readers, not from the person memorialized.

### Beat 01: The Sentence Starts with Hunger × starved

**Beat question:** When a reader meets “starved” during “The Sentence Starts with Hunger,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that a grave bodily outcome, not a noble image?

#### Scene draft 001 — starved — The Sentence Starts with Hunger

This version starts in the small interval between recognizing the record and deciding what to repeat from it. The dramatic work of “The Sentence Starts with Hunger” comes from placing “starved” beside what the memorial does not say. No invented detail is allowed to close that gap. The proposed reader is not a witness to the life being memorialized; their presence only gives the source wording a present audience.

Let the focus settle on “starved”. The phrase has force because a grave bodily outcome, not a noble image. A second person notices that force before anyone turns it into an explanation. They may look toward the line, then toward the person who has stopped reading, giving the beat a human action rather than an invented flashback.

A listener who values generosity but resists simplification begins with a modest response: “I can read starved, but I cannot tell from this sentence everything that happened. I want to keep the wording because it matters to someone, and I want to leave room where the record leaves room.” The line is written as present interpretation, not a quotation falsely attributed to the person on the memorial.

A keeper attentive to who has permission to call something a choice replies, “Then let us ask what it asks of us now. When a reader meets “starved” during “The Sentence Starts with Hunger,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that a grave bodily outcome, not a noble image? I can honor the claim and still notice what it does not tell us.” The answer should be specific to the source phrase; it must not flatten the disagreement into a lesson about all grief or all survivors.

A third voice, a reader who protects the dignity of the hungry, may interrupt gently. They do not correct the catalog or add evidence. They ask whether the word “starved” is being repeated because it comforts the room, because it warns the room, or because no one knows what else to say. Let more than one motive remain possible.

The scene’s emotional turn around “starved” in “The Sentence Starts with Hunger” is small: one listener stops trying to finish the missing story for a moment. A chair shifts, a copied line is turned face down and then face up again, or the speaker chooses a less certain verb. The selected gesture should fit the verified presentation context; no physical memorial site is established by this proposal.

For “starved” in “The Sentence Starts with Hunger,” player-facing tone stays open. The player may read, ask for the source wording again, decline the conversation, or simply listen. These are writing possibilities, not a promised menu or branching system. No answer earns approval, trust, resources, safety, reputation, or a new relationship state.

The closing breath belongs to the living reader; do not fabricate an answer on behalf of the person commemorated. The last attention returns to “starved”, now heard through the live disagreement rather than treated as a complete biography.

## 13. Player-facing tone

Keep sentences close to the source wording and the present reader’s actual knowledge. Allow one voice to value the memorial and another to object to its universal-sounding conclusion. Neither reaction should be framed as the correct morality. Use quiet material action rather than explanatory narration. Avoid turning illness, hunger, conflict, radiation, cold, machinery, duty, or trust into a puzzle tutorial or instruction to the player.

## 14. Editorial acceptance

Do not invent individual names, ration quantities, causes, duration, policy, food type, health effect, or proof of voluntary choice. Do not romanticize starvation, offer nutrition advice, or imply that memorial praise authorizes future deprivation. The record does not specify a food system or a playable choice.
 Re-check the exact local record and any newer narrative data before selecting text. Retain only passages that the current consumer can reach and attribute honestly. Shortening a candidate is preferable to implying unsupported canon.

## 15. Character and relationship continuity

The proposed roles are anonymous present-day readers. Do not promote them into named survivors, historical witnesses, patients, faction leaders, victims, or permanent companions without a separate source and authorized content decision. Their relationships exist only within the selected text. Gratitude, skepticism, grief, forgiveness, caution, and disagreement do not become saved relationship values.

## 16. Passage-selection map

**Use a scene** when a verified reader-facing owner can stage a present response without claiming a sourced location. **Use a record** when existing content supports authorship and provenance. **Use a conversation** when a second perspective adds a genuine question rather than repeating the thesis. **Use a return vignette** only if the existing route already supports the callback condition; otherwise use its non-conditional final line or omit it. These are writing choices, not feature requests.
